# Case Index

This index tracks case maturity and evidence type. A checked box in the roadmap is not enough: each case should show **what kind of claim has actually been established**.

## Status levels

### `scouted`

A potentially useful case has been identified, but its historical vocabulary and mechanism have not yet been verified.

### `first-pass`

The case has:

- a bounded research question;
- at least one credible historical or technical source;
- a mechanism-level description;
- explicit separation of historical record from modern analogy;
- known evidence gaps.

It is usable for comparison but should not be treated as settled.

### `grounded`

The case has:

- strong primary evidence where available;
- precise source locations;
- historical vocabulary;
- mechanism and failure modes;
- counterexamples / limits;
- related-repository duplication checked.

### `mature`

The case is strong enough to support synthesis across cases. It has survived source deepening and conceptual comparison, and its central claims no longer depend on a single source or fragile analogy.

---

## Evidence labels

Use these labels in case claim ledgers where useful:

| Label | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `H` | historical record |
| `P` | primary / contemporary evidence |
| `S` | scholarly or institutional secondary evidence |
| `E` | engineering / operational reconstruction |
| `A` | functional analogy |
| `I` | philosophical / media-theoretical interpretation |
| `X` | rejected or explicitly unsupported claim |

A claim may have more than one label, for example `H/P` or `E/A`.

## Comparison matrix — provisional

This matrix should become more precise as cases mature.

| Case | State substrate | Active retention work | Read | Addressing | Location stability | History retained by default? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Abacus / reckoning surface | bead/counter position + positional convention + procedural context | mostly human protection / interpretation / selection; no machine refresh merely to remain | nondestructive visual/manual | human-mediated spatial selection through positional convention | high during one configuration | no |
| Delay line | propagating pulse pattern | continuous circulation / regeneration / retiming + environmental timing/temperature correction | electronically sensed; state continues by recirculation | temporal slot + index | not meaningfully static | no |
| Magnetic core | remanent magnetization | little merely to remain at rest; active restore after classic destructive read | destructive in the bounded classic case, followed by rewrite when logical value must persist; contemporary nondestructive schemes also existed | coincident coordinate selection; nonselected cores must tolerate half-select disturbance | high at the selected core | no |
| Apollo AGC core rope / wired-in fixed memory | sense-wire thread/bypass geometry around ferrite cores + inhibit/address-selection wiring; core magnetic state is read transduction rather than the fixed payload bit | no periodic rewriting merely to preserve the fixed program; manufacturing/verification before service and module replacement for revision; selected core still undergoes set/reset switching during each read | logically nondestructive fixed-memory read even though the selected core changes magnetic state and is reset | fixed-memory address decoded through set/reset + inhibit selection, then selected sense-line group; addressability infrastructure is distinct from payload topology | high within one manufactured module; current program can change by replacing the module while an old module remains readable | no operational history by default; a superseded physical rope may preserve an obsolete complete program image |
| IBM System/360 Model 40 TROS / 1964–1970 bounded regime | printed control-tape drive-line routing through/bypass transformer positions + sense windings; fixed microinstruction/control-word state | no periodic payload refresh established; operating read current plus carrier/configuration integrity; deliberate revision by physical control-tape replacement | addressed drive-line pulse is inductively sensed; logically nondestructive with respect to the encoded tape route | ROAR/ROSCAR selects one fixed control word; fine addressability does not create runtime write authority | high within one installed tape set; current microprogram can change by physical carrier replacement while superseded tapes survive | no operation history by default; a removed carrier can preserve an obsolete complete microinstruction pattern |
| Coincident-current magnetic-core half-select / 1951–1959 bounded regime | remanent ferrite state + X/Y coordinate partial excitation + shared sense wiring + bounded inhibit control | no periodic refresh at rest; reliable operation requires non-target cores to tolerate repeated nonselecting pulses and the sense path to discriminate selected output from partial-select disturbance | selected read may remain destructive as in Case 02; half-selected neighbors can produce partial outputs without intended stable-state reversal | logical address selects an X/Y intersection/word while many cores sharing one coordinate are physically excited; inhibit can further qualify bit transitions | no payload relocation; physical effect scope extends beyond logical target | no payload history required, but material/sense margins must survive ordinary access traffic; disturbance cancellation is readout-path control rather than payload restoration |
| Apache ZooKeeper fuzzy snapshot / 2009–2019 bounded regime | in-memory data tree + non-volatile ordered transaction log + fuzzy snapshot + snapshot-start zxid + recovery-set file coverage | committed transactions are logged before application; snapshot scans without globally freezing the tree; recovery replays idempotent transactions in order; old recovery sets may later be purged by policy | serving reads use the in-memory replica; restart first reconstructs the tree from fuzzy materialization plus replay history | znode paths name logical state while zxid/log-file boundaries locate recovery progress rather than physical payload placement | snapshot payload can be temporally mixed; current logical tree survives process loss if the required snapshot/log closure remains | retains bounded replay history and optional older recovery sets rather than a mandatory complete indefinite operation history |
| DRAM | capacitor / storage-node charge | periodic regeneration because of leakage; shared row-level sense/restore infrastructure; restore after destructive read in the bounded Dennard 1T1C embodiment | destructive in the bounded Dennard embodiment; commercial dynamic memories can have nondestructive reads | stable row/column selection through decoders and shared sense/restore infrastructure | logical cell stable while physical charge is repeatedly renewed | no |
| JEDEC DDR4 refresh scheduling / 2012 bounded regime | volatile DRAM payload + device-internal refresh addressing + external refresh schedule + bounded postpone/pull-in relation + MR3 FGR mode | periodic refresh remains mandatory, but bounded work may shift earlier/later; 1x/2x/4x pair command frequency with different `tRFC`; some Self Refresh exits require catch-up work | each rank-level refresh blocks ordinary rank access for its applicable `tRFC` in the bounded FGR analysis; this case focuses on maintenance scheduling rather than cell-read destructiveness | normal data addressing is unchanged; `REF` uses internal refresh addressing while the controller retains/derives temporal schedule position | payload cell location stays fixed while maintenance windows move in time | no payload history; only bounded recent maintenance-accounting/grouping state is needed or derivable to know what work is owed/prepaid and which transition is legal |
| Mapped Flash | nonvolatile cell state + allocation/mapping metadata | out-of-place update, map maintenance, reclamation; wear equalization is a distinct optional objective; bad-block/ECC replacement appears in later bounded NAND evidence | map-mediated read of current physical embodiment | stable virtual/logical designation translated through retained mapping state | deliberately unstable under rewrite/reclaim | usually no; stale physical embodiments may cease to count before erase |
| NAND read disturb / 2002–2015 bounded regime (Case 52 canonical) | nonvolatile threshold-voltage state in NAND blocks/strings + ECC margin + optional read-count/voltage/mapping maintenance state | ordinary reads can cumulatively disturb unread neighbor cells through pass-through bias; mitigation can include ECC, Vpass tuning, read-count-triggered rewrite/migration, and erase renewal | logically nondestructive for the requested payload in the bounded sense, but physically non-neutral to unread cells sharing the NAND read path | page/block selection below an optional FTL; the logical target is narrower than the physically biased string/block neighborhood | payload can remain in place until mitigation rewrites/remaps it; relocation can preserve logical identity through Case-04-style mapping | no complete read history by default; bounded exposure/control summaries can be enough for maintenance decisions |
| RADOS | multiple object replicas + cluster map + PG/version/recovery state | replication, failure detection, peering, re-replication, migration/recovery | protocol-authorized current replica; exact read role depends on replication scheme | object → PG → CRUSH/current map → candidate OSD set, then currentness/authority checks | no permanently privileged physical home | no by default; PG logs retain bounded recovery history and can be guarded separately from every replica |
| Ceph unfound/lost / 2010–2014 bounded regime | retained missing/current-version relation + historical acting intervals + candidate OSD search set + surviving older versions/absence + administrator loss decision | automatic recovery keeps probing historically plausible sources; when newest payload remains unfound, final abandonment is explicitly operator-gated; `revert` or later `delete` resolves the blocked obligation | ordinary current-version recovery is unavailable at the failure boundary; forced `revert` admits an older value or absence rather than reconstructing the missing newest payload | current placement is insufficient; recovery uses historical PG acting intervals to enumerate OSDs that might still hold the required version | no recoverable newest embodiment may remain even though the logical system still retains an existence/currentness claim; later rollback/delete changes admissible continuation without proving media erasure | no complete operation history; bounded placement/version evidence and candidate-source accounting are retained long enough to decide whether recovery is still owed |
| HDFS Observer NameNode | journal-ordered namespace replica + Observer last-applied transaction frontier + client maximum-seen state ID | edit-log tailing advances replicas; state alignment waits/retries; `msync` refreshes the client frontier from Active; proxy can fall back to Active | selected coordinated reads may be served by a sufficiently caught-up Observer; writes/active-only operations remain Active-directed | client state ID is a lower bound on acceptable namespace progress, distinct from HA role and writer fencing | logical namespace identity survives while serving NameNode varies and Observer lag changes | bounded max-seen frontier only; it does not retain complete client or namespace history |
| Powered flip-flop / ENIAC + Whirlwind register witness | regenerative vacuum-tube circuit condition used as normal/abnormal machine state; Burks 1947 directly anchors two stable states and DC cross-coupling; ENIAC Part II adds staged unsynchronized/synchronized control state; Whirlwind adds grouped register-level organization | operating power and stable circuit conditions; explicit set/reset/clear; trigger/recovery timing and staged gating; no scheduled state refresh established in the bounded thermionic evidence | bounded ENIAC output/gate use is nondestructive; reset is separate | one flip-flop can be directly wired into later logic; Part-II control state can enable a later timing gate; counters/accumulators add higher organization; Whirlwind AR shows period register-level bus/gate/function organization | fixed circuit element while powered; architectural register role is not a location abstraction | no; working condition is current/pending state only |
| Static MOS RAM / Intel 1101A–2102–5101 + Pashley design boundary | powered DC-stable / bistable MOS cell state; Pashley 1975 directly grounds one Intel manufacturer-primary cross-feedback static-cell design, while exact commercial-model topology remains unassigned | continued retention-supporting supply; no scheduled clocks/refresh for cited static devices; 5101L exposes lower-power data-retention mode and operation recovery | cited Intel 2102/5101 reads are nondestructive; Pashley separately grounds sense-path and address-transition reliability constraints | monolithic array + X/Y or row/column selection + access devices + chip/interface control + sensing/I/O; Pashley makes the cell/decoder/sense/address-buffer layers explicit | fixed cell position within the bounded array; no location abstraction is claimed | no; current cell state only |
| IBM System/360 Model 85 cache | fast monolithic derivative copy + sector-address correspondence + per-block validity + activity-list policy state; authoritative value remains in main core storage under the bounded store policy | demand loading; processor/channel-store update of resident copies; validity reset and sector reassignment; activity-list revision; no periodic cache refresh established | cache fetch first tests correspondence + validity, then reads; miss loads from main storage | program-visible main-storage address resolved against hidden dynamic sector assignment and validity | cache residence deliberately changes while program-visible main-storage location remains stable | no; activity list retains bounded recency policy state, not application history |
| IBM store-in cache / 1971–1982 bounded regime | private-cache payload + directory/valid/modified and RO/EX currentness state + shared/central backing copy that may be stale | processor stores can create cache-local newer state; replacement or competing demand can trigger castout; controller qualifies backing-store currentness | local cache can supply the current line; a conflicting central-storage request can be redirected through castout then refetch in the 3081 | shared-memory address remains stable while cache directory/currentness state determines which embodiment may serve it and who may update it | current authority can reside temporarily in cache before castout makes central storage current again | no complete store history; bounded metadata retains current divergence/authority and the obligation to preserve a modified line before retirement |
| TI CBR-refresh DRAM | volatile dynamic payload state + on-chip refresh-counter control state | deadline-driven row reconstruction continues; external logic still causes refresh requests while on-chip counter supplies successive refresh-row addresses; hidden refresh can overlap retention work with valid output | ordinary read/write semantics remain device-specific; hidden refresh can preserve a valid output while refresh cycles proceed | normal accesses use external multiplexed addresses; CBR refresh ignores external address and uses internally generated refresh row | payload cells remain fixed; the **source of maintenance addressing** moves across the package boundary | no; refresh-counter state is bounded maintenance-control state, not application history |
| Toshiba leakage-tracked self-refresh | volatile dynamic payload + leak-monitor capacitor + threshold/control state + refresh counter | monitor state decays; threshold starts on-chip oscillator; counter walks refresh rows; completion recharges monitor; refresh frequency follows monitored leakage condition | ordinary product read/write behavior is outside this patent-bounded slice | refresh rows are generated by the on-chip counter during the self-refresh pass | payload cells remain fixed; both maintenance timing generation and row enumeration are internal to the disclosed control design | no; monitor/counter state is maintenance-control state, not application history |
| Intel/Frohman floating-gate EPROM | charge trapped on an insulated floating gate, expressed through transistor conduction/threshold behavior | no periodic refresh or continuous operating power merely to retain the bounded programmed charge; programming and erasure are exceptional state-changing interventions | patent states nondestructive read at stress below avalanche-programming condition | X/Y electrical selection for the disclosed array's read/program path; radiation erase belongs to a different physical control geometry | fixed storage element in the bounded array; no logical remapping is claimed | no; current programmed state only |
| Intel 2816 EEPROM | nonvolatile floating-gate product state; Intel product names HMOS-E FLOTOX while the companion Intel patent grounds a thin-oxide tunneling program/erase mechanism class without claiming exact product topology identity | no periodic refresh merely to hold state; deliberate erase/write uses 21 V VPP, millisecond timing, mode/control logic, and a finite erase/write cycling budget | ordinary read uses the lower-voltage fast service path, separate from erase/write | byte address participates in fine-grained erase/write; a separate chip-erase mode deliberately resets the full array | fixed product locations in the bounded array; no remapping is claimed | no value history by default; prior erase/write activity can still matter physically through finite endurance |
| Early Flash EEPROM / Toshiba–Intel 1980–1988 bounded regime | nonvolatile floating-gate state in one-transistor-per-bit designs with erase infrastructure shared across many cells / whole array | no periodic refresh merely to hold state; elevated-voltage erase/program, command sequencing, erase/program verification, retries, and preservation/reconstruction of unaffected logical state when system use is finer than erase scope | fine-grained ordinary addressed read; bounded sources keep read path distinct from state-changing high-field operations | read/program selection is finer than whole-array erase in the bounded Intel design; Toshiba also documents shared erase-gate groups | fixed cell positions; no logical remapping is claimed | no value history by default; program/erase cycling and verification state can affect future service |

| Apache Cassandra tombstone grace / compaction purge | user-value versions + timestamped deletion marker + local deletion time + grace/purge threshold + compaction overlap knowledge + distributed replica/reconciliation state | delete writes a negative marker; marker must continue to suppress older versions through compaction/replica lag; after grace and applicable closure conditions, compaction can retire obsolete marker/data | reads reject versions older than the tombstone; an unreconciled stale value can become visible again if deletion evidence disappears too early | timestamp ordering + marker presence qualify currentness; local overlap checks bound safe compaction purge; distributed correctness also depends on stale-replica reconciliation assumptions | old positive bytes may survive after logical deletion; correct reclamation can later discard both old values and the no-longer-needed negative marker | no complete operation history; a selective deletion/currentness witness is retained only while older state can still matter |
| DRAM RowHammer / targeted refresh | victim-cell charge + ordinary refresh cadence + aggressor/victim relation + optional access-count/threshold state + physical-adjacency mapping | ordinary timed refresh restores charge; repeated aggressor activation can accelerate victim leakage; stronger global refresh or targeted neighbor refresh adds workload/topology-conditioned maintenance | row activation restores the opened aggressor yet can indirectly endanger neighboring victims; once a victim value has corrupted, refresh alone is not evidence of original-payload reconstruction | refresh cadence plus disturbance-mitigation policy qualify reliability; physical adjacency/mapping knowledge matters for selective victim refresh | no authorized deletion path in the bounded case; a bit flip is unintended state change, not deliberate forgetting | no complete access history is required by PARA; counter-based alternatives retain bounded activity/threshold state rather than a full trace |
| DRAM VRT / retention-time profiling | payload charge + profiled row/cell retention class + refresh-policy state + measurement context | ordinary refresh restores charge; controller may reduce work using retained retention-time bins, but DPD/VRT can invalidate a previously safe classification | ordinary activation/read restoration remains DRAM semantics; the bounded case centers on whether the chosen future refresh deadline remains valid | profile/bin metadata can qualify a row for slower refresh only while conservative for the current physical/context state; revalidation or error tolerance may be required | no authorized deletion path; data can be lost because stale maintenance metadata permits too-long a restoration interval | no complete leakage/history trace is retained; a compressed classification survives while hidden physical VRT state or data-pattern context can change |
| RAID-6 P+Q / 1993–2011 bounded regime | data blocks + independent P XOR and Q finite-field syndromes + member-position/failure-location relation | update both P/Q; reconstruct one/two known missing contributions; rebuild/restore full margin separately; preserve syndrome currentness | surviving direct reads remain ordinary; known missing contributions are reconstructed; arbitrary dual corruption is not generally self-locating | stripe/member index determines Q coefficient; recovery additionally needs which positions failed | physical members may be replaced while the logical stripe contribution is reconstructed; the code relation remains usable only while P/Q/currentness/position relations stay valid | no complete history; retains current coded constraints plus bounded layout/failure/currentness state needed by the recovery model |
| ZFS RAID-Z dynamic-stripe COW / 2005–2010 bounded regime | committed block tree + variable-width RAID-Z data/parity columns + block-pointer/redundancy geometry + transaction authority | allocate/write new COW blocks; calculate a complete parity relation for the selected dynamic stripe; retain metadata needed to interpret geometry; commit/select the new tree; reclaim older embodiments later | ordinary reads are outside this bounded write-hole slice; reconstruction still depends on geometry and separate checksum/integrity qualification | filesystem block pointer resolves logical block to vdev/RAID-Z layout whose width is not globally fixed | old committed embodiment can remain separately admissible while new physical sectors/columns are written; later reclamation can retire the old embodiment | no complete write history by default; retains current committed tree/geometry relations rather than a PPL-style repair log, while uncommitted newest async state may be lost |
| OpenZFS dRAID / 2017–2021 bounded recovery regime | surviving data/parity + fixed-width redundancy-group geometry + deterministic permutation mapping + distributed spare regions + space-map/rebuild progress + later block-pointer/checksum authority | after device loss, reconstruct allocated ranges sequentially in LBA order across many children into distributed spare capacity; restore coded redundancy first; run later scrub/healing verification | sequential reconstruction reads surviving coded contributions without per-block checksum verification in the first phase; later scrub re-enters block-pointer/checksum space | logical blocks map through fixed-width dRAID groups and deterministic permutations; recovery traversal can use space-map/device-address order rather than full block-tree traversal | distributed spare regions become recovery destinations; replacement/rebalancing can later move state again while restoring spare availability | does not preserve a complete mutation history; preserves layout/rebuild state sufficient to restore missing contributions, then separately revalidates integrity |
| ZFS DTL / 2005–2007 selective-resilver regime | surviving redundant blocks + per-block birth txg + per-target failed-replication DTL intervals + tree/topology state | retain exposure interval while a target misses writes; after return, compare current block birth txgs with DTL; resilver matching blocks and prune branches that cannot intersect the interval; retire repair evidence after recovery conditions permit | ordinary source reads/checksums remain separate; DTL membership identifies possible replica incompleteness, not corruption proof | block tree supplies location/reachability while birth txg supplies temporal membership in repair scope | same logical target/device can return and be caught up without full replacement; replacement presents a larger trust/repair boundary | retains a bounded failure-exposure summary rather than a complete mutation history; later OpenZFS can persist a sufficient leaf-missing basis and derive higher-level DTL state |

---

## Cross-case findings already supported

After ninety-four bounded cases, **all ninety-four cases are now `grounded`.** The repository satisfies both the numeric and mechanism-variety gates for bounded synthesis. This does **not** make the provisional theses conclusions: new technical bridges must remain free to break or revise the current relational criterion.

1. **state retention ≠ history retention** — all cases preserve current working state without automatically preserving the complete sequence that produced it;
2. **retention mechanism ≠ apparent persistence** — an abacus configuration can sit still, a delay-line pattern survives by continual circulation, a core can remain magnetized at rest, a DRAM cell survives for a bounded interval before scheduled regeneration, mapped Flash can preserve a logical object while relocating its physical embodiment, and RADOS can preserve an object while replica membership changes;
3. **passive positional retention can be operational without autonomous machine readout** — Cheng's 1592 procedure directly couples designated abacus positions with an instruction to leave the completed numerical configuration unmoved, while Ries's 1525 line reckoning independently shows a positional counter field used as part of arithmetic;
4. **the retained state may be a relation rather than an isolated token** — in positional calculation, counter + position + convention + procedure constitute the actionable state; later mapped/distributed systems automate increasingly large parts of comparable identity relations;
5. **the degree to which a medium constrains legal states matters** — a bead frame constrains movement/position more strongly than an open line-reckoning surface even though both can support passive positional working retention;
6. **historical material operation and modern reconstruction must remain layered** — early Chinese procedural texts establish placement/position language, while exact counting-rod manipulation sometimes remains a specialist reconstruction rather than a directly described artifact operation;
7. **cross-cultural functional similarity ≠ genealogy** — Cheng 1592 and Ries 1525 can support a shared mechanism comparison without proving transmission, common origin, or modern-register ancestry;
8. **identity of logical state ≠ identity of physical token** — delay-line regeneration, destructive-read core restore, DRAM regeneration, mapped Flash, and RADOS recovery all preserve logical sameness through changed physical state;
9. **idle nonvolatility ≠ read invariance** — the grounded magnetic-core evidence shows that an element can retain remanent state without maintenance energy yet the bounded classic read operation can destroy that state and require rewrite;
10. **access can itself create a retention obligation** — classic destructive-read core and Dennard's bounded 1T1C embodiment may require immediate rewrite after read;
11. **destructive read is a regime, not an essence of magnetic core** — contemporary 1953–1954 nondestructive sensing/readout work bounds the classic destructive-read case and rejects `all core reads are destructive`;
12. **time can itself create a retention obligation** — DRAM adds a distinct deadline: charge leakage requires scheduled regeneration even without useful access;
13. **dynamic retention ≠ destructive read** — Dennard disclosed nondestructive alternatives; Intel 1103 manufacturer documentation combines dynamic storage and periodic refresh with nondestructive read; AMD's 1976 Am9050 directly combines a one-transistor/capacitor cell, nondestructive read, and mandatory refresh;
14. **identity persistence ≠ location persistence** — Ban's Flash mapping explicitly keeps logical unit identity stable while the physical location changes; Intel's 1995 FTL description independently presents logical-to-physical remapping as the mechanism by which a virtual block service survives Flash erase geometry; RADOS extends this across independently failing devices;
15. **logical invalidation ≠ physical erasure** — a block can cease to count as current (`deleted`, `dirty`, or invalidated) before the containing Flash erase unit is physically erased;
16. **metadata can be constitutive of retention** — in mapped Flash, maps/allocation state identify the current embodiment; in RADOS, cluster-map, placement, version, and recovery state help establish which replicas currently count;
17. **maintenance can be space/reclaim-triggered** — a nonvolatile medium may still require deferred copying and erasure so repeated logical rewrites can continue;
18. **reclamation ≠ wear leveling** — reclamation recovers writable capacity while preserving current data; Wells's 1992-lineage wear-leveling patent adds the distinct objective of distributing switching/erase burden to extend usable medium life;
19. **historical terminology must follow the source** — Ban's 1993-filed system uses `virtual map` / logical-unit vocabulary; Intel AP-619 documents `Flash Translation Layer (FTL)` as a PCMCIA-approved format by August 1995. Earlier mechanisms should not be retroactively renamed without evidence;
20. **replica multiplicity ≠ retained currentness** — several physical copies may exist while only a version-consistent subset represents the current ordered state;
21. **maintenance can be failure/repair-triggered** — distributed redundancy can degrade after failure or membership change and be restored by reconstructing current state onto replacement members; bounded 1998 NAND evidence also shows block replacement as a local device-management response to program/erase failure;
22. **logical success ≠ durable commit** — the bounded 2006 RADOS design distinguishes replicated in-memory acknowledgement from later persistent-media commit, and the contemporaneous 2007 dissertation preserves this distinction across the expanded replication discussion;
23. **retention of currentness metadata can be guarded separately from every material replica** — the 2007 RADOS paper explicitly protects PG logs describing what a PG should contain even while object replicas may remain missing during background recovery;
24. **readability ≠ authorized currentness** — the 2007 RADOS design makes map-epoch and heartbeat state part of deciding whether an otherwise reachable replica may safely answer a read;
25. **refresh can be shared reconstruction, not merely a timer event** — commercial DRAM documentation makes row selection, sense amplification, restoration, and return-to-cell part of maintaining a large array of minimal storage cells;
26. **retention ≠ addressability** — a state can remain physically present while the selector, map, convention, or index needed to choose it is absent; passive positional retention can also be operationally selected by a human without autonomous machine addressing;
27. **address stability ≠ location stability** — DRAM preserves a stable cell-selection relation across repeated reconstruction, while mapped Flash and RADOS preserve higher-level designations across deliberate physical relocation or replica replacement;
28. **address resolution ≠ currentness authorization** — mapped Flash can leave an old embodiment physically present after logical invalidation, and RADOS can resolve a reachable replica that is stale or not currently authorized to answer;
29. **selection machinery can be retention machinery** — magnetic-core half-select margins/destructive read and DRAM sense/restore show that the way a state is selected can create disturbance or reconstruction obligations rather than merely revealing an independent stored value;
30. **addressability is not one historical scalar** — human spatial selection, coordinate decoding, logical translation, and distributed placement are different operational relations with different costs; the repository should not narrate them as a simple monotonic ascent from `less` to `more` addressable;
31. **physical-token continuity ≠ physical-home continuity** — classic core restore and bounded DRAM regeneration can replace the immediate magnetic/electrical state while continuing to use the same selected physical location;
32. **location can be constitutive of a retained identity** — positional calculation is a counterexample to any universal `logical state is location-independent` thesis because position participates directly in the operative numerical meaning;
33. **relocation ≠ immateriality** — mapped Flash and RADOS let a logical identity survive replacement of physical embodiments, but every current embodiment remains material and subject to device/topology constraints;
34. **removing one permanent home can increase dependence on retained relations** — Flash mapping/allocation state and RADOS placement/version/currentness state become constitutive precisely because physical embodiments are replaceable;
35. **physical topology remains relevant after location abstraction** — RADOS uses failure-domain topology in placement, so absence of one permanent home does not make `where replicas are` irrelevant to durability;
36. **permanent physical home ≠ temporary protocol authority** — RADOS can move replicas and primary/read roles while still requiring protocol-defined authority over which state may order or answer as current;
37. **mobility can create maintenance rather than eliminate it** — Flash relocation creates map/reclamation obligations and distributed replica replacement creates peering/repair obligations;
38. **the location-detachment sequence is functional, not teleological** — fixed cells, stable addresses, remapped blocks, and distributed replicas can coexist in one modern stack; the audit's stages do not establish one inevitable historical ascent toward placeless storage;
39. **technical forgetting is layer-relative** — a forgetting claim must name whether the lost target is a physical distinction, logical value/identity, mapping/currentness relation, serviceability, history, or durability threshold;
40. **physical loss ≠ higher-level forgetting** — ordinary DRAM reconstruction replaces charge, Flash reclamation can erase an obsolete embodiment, and RADOS can replace a failed replica while preserving the logical state;
41. **physical survival ≠ retained current state** — an uninterpretable positional configuration, an invalidated Flash block, or a stale/deauthorized RADOS replica can remain materially present after it has ceased to function as the intended current state;
42. **missed maintenance can be a forgetting mechanism, but maintenance triggers differ** — destructive core read creates an access-triggered restore obligation, DRAM creates a deadline-triggered refresh obligation, and distributed systems create failure/repair-triggered obligations;
43. **relation loss can matter as much as payload loss** — positional convention/procedure, Flash mapping/allocation, and RADOS version/PG-log/currentness state can be required to recover which surviving state is meaningful or current;
44. **unavailability, staleness, logical deletion, and physical erasure are not synonyms** — each can occur without some of the others, and temporary loss of service should not be counted as technical forgetting merely because the state cannot be used at one moment;
45. **maintenance visibility is observer-relative** — a DRAM refresh can be absent from an application's semantic model while remaining a first-class timing/design obligation; RADOS repair can be transparent to an object client while highly visible to operators;
46. **automation ≠ invisibility** — a system can automate restore, refresh, remapping, or repair while exposing timing, degraded state, capacity consumption, diagnostics, or implementation constraints to another layer;
47. **interface invisibility ≠ labor disappearance** — core manufacturing/support work and distributed replacement/infrastructure remain material and institutional even when a logical memory/object interface hides lower-layer operations;
48. **nonvolatility can reduce one maintenance obligation while leaving others intact** — core remanence and Flash quiescence remove periodic refresh merely to keep an idle physical state present, but access, mapping, reclaim, wear, failure, and system-support obligations remain;
49. **reliability is not one cross-period scalar** — positional disturbance, half-select/sense error, missed refresh, Flash wear/block failure, and replica/currentness failure are different failure models and cannot be ranked by one unsupported `more reliable` scale;
50. **self-healing ≠ maintenance-free** — automated distributed repair still depends on surviving current state, functioning control machinery, spare capacity, replacement members, and physical failure-domain assumptions;
51. **stable interfaces can relocate retention work** — classic core hides read–rewrite in the memory cycle, DRAM hides scheduled reconstruction below ordinary software, mapped Flash hides physical relocation behind logical designation, and RADOS hides replica replacement behind the object service;
52. **work displacement is functional, not teleological** — visible human procedure, device/controller work, protocol repair, manufacturing, and operations coexist in modern stacks; the evidence does not establish a historical law that later systems necessarily hide more maintenance or use less human labor;
53. **operational analysis ≠ continuous-retention ontology** — Ernst's demand to inspect what a technical medium does is methodologically strong, but quiescent positional, core, and Flash states reject the inference that a retained state must be continuously acted upon merely to remain;
54. **retention-time operation ≠ access-time operation** — a core can retain remanence without power yet require a timed sense/rewrite sequence on access; a passive positional state can remain while selection and interpretation occur only when a human returns to it;
55. **microtemporality is mechanism-dependent rather than universal** — pulse phase, sense timing, and DRAM refresh deadlines make short timescales causally decisive in some mechanisms, while Flash reclamation and RADOS repair may be deferred or event-triggered on longer operational horizons;
56. **quiescence is itself a temporal regime** — `nothing must happen yet` is an engineering fact when a mechanism lacks a recurring reconstruction deadline under specified conditions; quiescent retention is not absence of temporality;
57. **technical retention has plural operational timescales** — continuous circulation, access-triggered restore, deadline-driven refresh, workload/capacity/wear triggers, failure/membership repair, and human procedural continuity should not be collapsed into one generic `time-critical` rhythm;
58. **philosophical fit cannot promote evidence maturity** — delay-line circulation is especially congruent with Ernstian microtemporality, but the case was promoted only after exact patent locations, directly inspected period-paper evidence, and machine-specific primary temperature-control evidence closed its source-grounding blockers;
59. **technical retention is broader than Stieglerian tertiary retention** — a retained machine state can be technically real and analytically important without thereby participating in the thicker relation of exteriorization, repetition, learning, or transmission that the bounded Stiegler test tracks;
60. **substrate class does not decide philosophical class** — volatile DRAM, nonvolatile Flash, and replicated objects can all support tertiary-retentional traces, while arbitrary internal states on the same substrates need not play the same retentional role;
61. **retentional object ≠ all constitutive retention infrastructure** — mappings, currentness/version state, refresh, placement, and repair can be required to sustain an exteriorized trace without being philosophically identical to that trace;
62. **Bestand ≠ storage** — Heidegger's primary text places storing within a larger chain of transformation/distribution/switching and treats standing-reserve as a mode of orderability rather than a noun for stored items;
63. **technical availability ≠ Heideggerian orderability** — designation, resolution, currentness/admissibility, and recovery can explain whether a system can service a request, but that engineering relation does not by itself establish Heidegger's mode of revealing;
64. **mere later usability is not enough for standing-reserve** — the grounded abacus case is a negative control: an intentionally retained state can be selected for a later operation without licensing the claim that any usable retained state is Heideggerian `Bestand`;
65. **replaceable embodiment can increase flexible callability without producing immateriality** — mapped Flash and RADOS preserve logical service through changing embodiments, but that service remains dependent on physical media/topology and retained mapping, placement, authority, and repair relations;
66. **physical presence ≠ orderable currentness** — an invalidated Flash embodiment or stale/deauthorized RADOS replica can survive materially and even be readable while no longer counting as the state that the current interface may order or return;
67. **forensic materiality ≠ a universal remanence law** — Kirschenbaum's materiality discipline remains useful beyond disk, but no mechanism-level evidence supports the claim that every obsolete digital state remains indefinitely recoverable merely because it once had a physical embodiment;
68. **interface disappearance ≠ raw-media absence** — grounded Flash already separates logical invalidation from later physical erase, and the bounded FAST 2011 SSD comparison experimentally shows that FTL indirection can leave raw-Flash remnants invisible through the normal host interface;
69. **forensic witness ≠ authoritative current state** — an obsolete Flash embodiment or stale RADOS replica can preserve evidence of an earlier state without being the embodiment that mapping/version/currentness rules authorize as current;
70. **logical-object survivability ≠ current-embodiment survivability ≠ forensic-trace survivability** — remapping and replica replacement can preserve the logical object while earlier embodiments disappear, while stale traces can also survive after they cease to participate in current service;
71. **logical repeatability can be achieved through material nonidentity** — stable Flash designations and RADOS object identities can return what counts as the same logical state across changed physical locations or replica membership, so repeatability does not imply continuity of one material token;
72. **physical survival ≠ forensic accessibility** — recoverability of a surviving embodiment depends on the medium, controller state, reclamation history, instrumentation, encryption/key relations, and the relevant adversary/interface rather than on material persistence alone;
73. **distributed replication can multiply material witnesses without multiplying current authority** — RADOS replicas may have different individualized material histories while protocol version/epoch/peering rules still establish one admissible current state for service;
74. **technical retention ≠ one physical or engineering mechanism** — the grounded cases share no common remanence, refresh, mapping, replication, address, or maintenance operation; any mechanism-specific definition already has direct counterexamples;
75. **technical retention ≠ generic physical persistence or causal residue** — material survival alone is insufficient because positional meaning can be lost while configuration survives, and Flash/RADOS embodiments can survive after losing current logical status or authority;
76. **the current minimal cross-case invariant is target-relative admissible continuation across time** — analysis must name a retention target, temporal separation, continuity mechanism, later recovery/interpretation/admissibility operation, and a sameness/currentness rule explaining why the later state counts as the target;
77. **recoverability is target-relative** — a stale Flash page or old RADOS replica may be recoverable as a forensic/historical trace while not being recoverable as the current operational object, so `can be recovered` is incomplete without `recovered as what`;
78. **technical retention has no currently justified minimum duration** — grounded ENIAC flip-flop evidence now survives the adversarial short-duration test: states established and reused on microsecond-scale margins can still make later behavior depend on whether the earlier state remained; no arbitrary durability threshold is justified by the cases;
79. **one controlled relation does not imply one natural technology kind** — the grounded mechanisms are better compared through cross-cutting axes such as continuity target, maintenance trigger, embodiment replacement, recovery performer, and currentness/interpretation rule than forced into mutually exclusive `passive/active/distributed` subfamilies;
80. **category coherence is provisional and evidence-gated** — forty-nine grounded regimes now support the current relational criterion, including the grounded delay-line circulation/temperature-control regime plus powered flip-flop, static-MOS, cache-policy, refresh-address-internalization, autonomous leakage-tracked refresh-scheduling, SDRAM refresh-mode handoff, floating-gate EPROM erase-asymmetry, byte-erasable EEPROM, coarse-erase early Flash, HDD defect-reassignment, SSD power-loss-durability, BSD FFS crash-admissibility, RAID parity-reconstruction, ZFS proactive-scrubbing, f4 distributed-erasure-coding, NVMe 1.0 persistence-interface, IBM paging/backing-copy-currentness, Dynamo divergent-version/anti-entropy, Windows Azure LRC repair-locality/representation-handoff, Swift mutable-EC currentness, GFS distributed-integrity-verification, Ceph EC checksum-authority/deep-scrub, Swift distributed-delete/tombstone-consistency, Ceph Luminous scrub-repair-authority, NVMe 1.4 persistent-memory-region, SNIA persistence-domain, Intel ADR/eADR power-fail-domain, DDR5 same-bank-refresh-localization, temperature-conditioned DRAM-refresh, commercial Mobile-DDR automatic-TCSR/selective-retention, NAND-Flash FCR controller-maintenance, commercial Samsung 840 EVO old-data performance-refresh, Intel DC S3700/S3500 PLI-health/validation, GeckoFTL controller-metadata-recovery, RAIDR retention-profile/row-selective-refresh, Cassandra tombstone/repair-window, Cassandra incremental-repair-state/anti-compaction/pending-repair, Kafka log-compaction/delete-marker-retention, AVATAR runtime-VRT-aware ECC/scrub refresh-feedback, NVMe 1.3 deallocate/sanitize-forgetting, FAST '11 SSD sanitization-verification/hidden-remnant, Micron DDR5 on-die-ECC/ECS corrective-scrub, and GFS master-log/checkpoint/re-derived-location recovery bridges; future write-back-cache, filesystem, refresh, virtual-memory, distributed, and log-compaction regimes must still be allowed to break or revise it rather than being forced into it;
81. **short duration ≠ trivial retention** — ENIAC's decade flip-flop can preserve a pending carry condition across intervening pulse activity until a later reset/carry phase; the operational difference between `state remained` and `state failed to remain` is real even when the interval is tiny compared with archival storage;
82. **continuous power ≠ periodic state maintenance** — the bounded thermionic flip-flop requires operating power for its regenerative state to exist, but the primary evidence does not establish a DRAM-like deadline-driven rewrite or a delay-line-like circulation operation merely to hold either state;
83. **volatility ≠ dynamic refresh** — ENIAC power-up can yield accidental flip-flop states and requires initialization, yet the bounded evidence does not show periodic refresh of a correctly powered flip-flop; loss on power interruption and deadline-driven reconstruction are different properties;
84. **later retention can be demonstrated by state-sensitive use rather than discrete retrieval** — ENIAC's static outputs and gate-control uses show that retained state may remain continuously exposed to downstream circuitry and matter when a later operation acts on it, so the cross-case criterion should not require a separate storage-style read transaction;
85. **flip-flop mechanism ≠ register architecture** — Eccles–Jordan's primary vocabulary is relay/amplification/retroaction, while ENIAC distinguishes flip-flops, ring counters, and accumulators; later `register` terminology must be sourced rather than used as a synonym for any bistable element;
86. **initialization is part of usable working retention** — ENIAC's random power-up states show that possessing a bistable physical state is not sufficient for computation; clearing establishes a system-admissible starting state before later continuity can be interpreted as retained working state;
87. **period `register` vocabulary names organization/role rather than one physical substrate class** — Whirlwind R-221 calls AR a `simple flip-flop register` while also using `storage register` for magnetic-core storage; period terminology therefore supports an architectural comparison but not `register = one device technology`;
88. **implementation relation ≠ synonymy** — Whirlwind supplies positive period evidence that a register can be implemented with flip-flops, while ENIAC and Whirlwind together still reject `every flip-flop = register`; the correct comparison keeps elementary state mechanism and architectural grouping/use distinct;
89. **state element, organization, architectural role, and interface semantics are separate comparison axes** — R-221's AR is not defined only by what holds a bistable state but by grouping, bus/gate connectivity, operand holding, transfer, sign sensing, and arithmetic use; later SRAM/cache work should not collapse these layers;
90. **source locator ≠ direct source inspection** — recovering the exact 1919 Eccles–Jordan periodical locators and ENIAC PX-1-105 drawing identity improves source control but does not justify page-image or component/topology claims until the relevant facsimile/schematic is directly inspected; evidence maturity remains claim-specific;
91. **the retained target can be a pending event condition, not only a numeric value** — ENIAC Part II p. IV-43 directly documents an incoming switch pulse setting an `unsynchronized flip-flop`; that state survives to enable a synchronizing gate so a later central programming pulse can establish a `synchronized flip-flop`, after which transmitter action and reset occur. The retention role is therefore tied to preserving that an event has occurred until a machine-timed consequence can safely follow;
92. **timing qualification can require staged retention without licensing modern terminology as history** — the same Part-II passage explains that an unsynchronized arrival can otherwise produce a reduced-magnitude pulse and unreliable operation, while the second retained stage makes the transmitter gate fully on/off for a later central program pulse. This supports a mechanism-level comparison to later synchronization problems, but `clock-domain crossing`, setup/hold, and metastability remain modern analogies unless separately sourced;
93. **regenerative retention ≠ persistence of the input stimulus** — the contemporary British Association account describes an electrical stimulus initiating a back-coupled chain in which valve current changes reinforce one another and there is `no restoring influence`; the later limiting circuit condition is therefore produced by the circuit's regenerative relation, not by the stimulus simply remaining present;
94. **a source gap can become archival cleanup without becoming visually verified** — the 1919 mechanism is now independently supported by GB148582A and the British Association proceedings, so the exact _Electrician_ / _Radio Review_ facsimile is no longer the sole mechanism anchor; nevertheless no figure, typography, or exact-page wording should be claimed from a page image that has not been rendered;
95. **state-holding feedback ≠ transition/recovery path** — Burks 1947 explicitly separates steady-state stability, governed by the direct-current cross-coupled relation, from flipping/triggering and recovery-to-ready dynamics, so one retained-state element can have different mechanisms/constraints for holding and changing state;
96. **short retention still has internal timing structure** — Burks reports roughly 1 µs to set and 4 µs to be ready for reset, while ENIAC gives at least 2.5 µs for setting and does not reset sooner than 10 µs after set; microsecond-scale retention therefore still has explicit engineering margins and recovery obligations;
97. **published primary schematic ≠ original production drawing** — Burks's directly inspected 1947 simplified ENIAC schematic is sufficient to ground the bounded bistability mechanism when combined with the primary report corpus, but it does not license PX-1-105-specific claims while that original drawing remains uninspected;
98. **static retention ≠ unpowered retention** — Intel's 2102 documentation couples `no clocks or refreshing required` to a powered +5 V static RAM, while 5101 documentation explicitly invokes battery backup when non-volatility is required; absence of scheduled refresh does not imply persistence without an electrical support condition;
99. **retention-supporting power ≠ active-operation power** — Intel's 5101L variants specify ordinary operation at 5 V while separately guaranteeing data retention down to 2.0 V and defining operation recovery; `needs power` is therefore too coarse a cross-case descriptor;
100. **battery-backed continuity ≠ intrinsic media nonvolatility** — the period 5101 documentation treats battery backup as a way to obtain non-volatility from a static semiconductor memory whose retained state still depends on continued supply;
101. **cell bistability ≠ array-memory semantics** — Vadasz et al. distinguish MOS storage arrays from sensing/decoding/drive support, Intel 2102 documentation separates the cell array from row/column selection, chip enable, input control, and output, and Pashley's Intel filing directly separates bistable cells from X/Y decoding, column sensing, read bus, write path, and address buffers;
102. **static ≠ timeless** — 1101A and 5101 documentation specifies read/write/select/setup/hold/recovery timing even though the bounded static devices need no periodic refresh merely to keep their state; Pashley adds address-transition and sensing timing/reliability constraints;
103. **period static-MOS evidence must not be normalized into a universal modern 6T-SRAM topology** — the 1970 NASA report supplies a generic cross-coupled static-MOS cell witness, Fairchild supplies a distinct early cell family, and Pashley supplies an Intel n-channel depletion-load design; none licenses the claim that every early static RAM was the later canonical complementary-CMOS 6T cell;
104. **product-specific period secondary evidence ≠ manufacturer-primary mechanism evidence** — Sideris's 1973 *Electronics* figure explicitly labels an Intel `1101 CELL` and treats it as static, narrowing the Intel-specific source gap, but the trade-journal drawing is not thereby an Intel production schematic or a directly inspected mask-level disclosure;
105. **artifact provenance ≠ artifact interpretation** — the Smithsonian catalogs an Intel-supplied 1101 RAM mask dated February 1972, establishing a surviving product-specific manufacturing artifact and a concrete archival route, but the catalog record alone does not identify the mask layer or justify a bit-cell topology reconstruction;
106. **source gaps should be typed by evidence class** — Pashley's 1975 Intel filing now closes the broad manufacturer-primary static-cell design gap for a `1024 × 1`, `32 × 32` Intel static RAM class, while exact 1101/1101A/2102/2102A product identity and exact mask interpretation remain narrower artifact/genealogy questions;
107. **manufacturer-primary mechanism evidence ≠ commercial-product identity** — an Intel-assigned patent can directly ground an Intel static-RAM cell/array mechanism without proving that the disclosed preferred embodiment is the 2102, 2102A, 1101, or 1101A; resemblance in capacity, organization, voltage, date, or inventor context is not enough to assign a model number;
108. **cell-state retention ≠ access-path reliability** — Pashley's design treats the bistable cell separately from column capacitance, sense amplification, read-bus loading, and output buffering, so a held state can remain physically valid while the path needed to recover it becomes too slow or unreliable;
109. **selection integrity ≠ hold margin** — the Pashley address buffer is designed so an address bit and its complement switch together, preventing unintended multiple selection; this is a retrieval/selection constraint rather than evidence that the unselected bistable cell has lost its state;
110. **same device count ≠ same historical topology** — the disclosed Intel depletion-load n-channel cell has two load devices, two drive/storage devices, and two access devices, but that six-device count does not make it the later complementary-CMOS `6T SRAM` cell or justify importing later device behavior backward;
111. **cache-copy retention ≠ authoritative-state retention** — the Model 85 updates main storage on every store and treats the cache as a transparent derivative copy, so reassignment can end fast-copy residency without ending the current architectural memory state;
112. **cache validity/currentness ≠ physical payload presence** — Liptay's fetch path requires both sector-address correspondence and a per-block `validity bit` before cache data may answer; a block can cease to count as resident by validity/correspondence state without this source proving either physical erasure or forensic survival of the old payload pattern;
113. **currentness metadata can be constitutive of fast service even with one authoritative copy** — distributed disagreement is not required for currentness to matter: the Model 85 must retain which main-storage sector a cache sector represents and whether each block under that assignment is loaded/current enough to serve;
114. **cache currentness maintenance can be event-triggered** — processor/channel stores update already resident copies, misses load demanded blocks, and reassignment resets validity; this differs from DRAM deadline refresh and RADOS failure-triggered repair;
115. **replacement-policy state ≠ retained application history** — the Model 85 activity list retains recency ordering sufficient to choose a victim sector and changes future fast-copy residency, but it is control state rather than a general record of the program's past;
116. **cache substrate ≠ cache semantics** — Liptay's fast monolithic store becomes a cache through transparent correspondence, validity, demand loading, update, and replacement rules; bistable/static storage technology by itself does not establish the cache role;
117. **modern cache taxonomy must not overwrite period terminology** — `sector address register`, `validity bit`, `activity list`, and `high-speed buffer` are directly sourced Model 85 terms; `tag-like`, `write-through-like`, `LRU-like`, and coherence analogies are useful modern classifications only when explicitly labeled;
118. **refresh obligation ≠ refresh-address-generation locus** — the TMS4256/TMS4257 family retains a 4 ms refresh deadline while CAS-before-RAS mode ignores the external address and generates the refresh row internally; the physical maintenance requirement and the source of the maintenance address are therefore separable;
119. **internalized refresh addressing ≠ autonomous refresh scheduling** — TI's 1984-filed refresh-counter patent explicitly states that the processor or memory controller external to the memory device controls how often CAS-before-RAS refresh requests occur, even though the row count is on-chip;
120. **hidden refresh ≠ absence of retention work** — TI documents hidden refresh as cycling RAS through refresh behavior while valid output data can remain at the output pin; maintenance can be hidden relative to one interface while still occurring materially and temporally;
121. **retention infrastructure can retain control state of its own** — the on-chip refresh counter carries a current row-enumeration state across successive refresh requests; it is not application payload or history, but it helps make coverage of the row set possible;
122. **period `self refresh` vocabulary ≠ later autonomous-self-refresh semantics** — US4653030A is titled `Self refresh circuitry for dynamic memory`, yet its bounded embodiment still requires externally timed CAS-before-RAS requests; historical terminology must be interpreted through the sourced mechanism rather than normalized backward from later standards;
123. **maintenance responsibility can migrate without the maintenance deadline moving** — Case 03 grounds the leakage deadline; Case 09 shows row enumeration moving from an externally supplied address toward an on-chip counter while the same class of deadline-driven reconstruction remains, making `where the work is performed` a separate comparison axis;
124. **refresh-address internalization ≠ refresh-schedule internalization** — Case 09 grounds an internal row counter with externally controlled refresh cadence, while Toshiba's bounded 1984-priority design places an oscillator/trigger path on-chip as well; `who names the next row` and `who decides when maintenance begins` are separate architecture questions;
125. **autonomous maintenance can be condition-derived rather than fixed-rate** — Toshiba's leak-current monitor capacitor is allowed to decay until a detector threshold starts the oscillator/counter refresh sequence; the maintenance event can therefore be derived from a monitored technical condition rather than only a fixed externally supplied cadence;
126. **retention infrastructure can use a deliberately decaying proxy state** — the Toshiba monitor capacitor is not payload, yet its decay is constitutive of deciding when payload preservation work begins; the patent even permits slightly greater monitor leakage than ordinary cells to create a conservative margin;
127. **maintenance-frequency optimization ≠ maintenance elimination** — the Toshiba design seeks lower standby power by avoiding refresh that is more frequent than required by the monitored condition, but a threshold event still starts a full row-refresh pass; less work under favorable conditions does not make dynamic storage quiescent or nonvolatile;
128. **manufacturer-primary disclosed mechanism ≠ product identity or invention priority** — US4682306A grounds the Toshiba circuit it describes, not a named commercial product, and the patent itself identifies Hitachi 59-56291 as earlier automatic refresh-frequency-control prior art;
129. **nonvolatility ≠ immutability** — Frohman's floating-gate device is explicitly designed for long-term storage without continuous power, yet the same patent provides radiation methods for removing the retained charge; survival of ordinary power loss does not mean the state lacks an erase path;
130. **read mechanism ≠ program mechanism** — US3744036A separates an avalanche-injection write cycle from a lower-stress read cycle and explicitly calls the read nondestructive; later use can inspect a retained state without reproducing the event that created it;
131. **read/program addressability ≠ erase authority or erase geometry** — the Intel array uses X/Y electrical selection for program/read, while the bounded floating-gate device disclosure removes charge by radiation applied to the transistor/device. Selection granularity for access therefore need not determine the physical geometry of forgetting;
132. **electrical programmability ≠ electrical erasability** — the bounded Intel/Frohman lineage electrically programs the floating gate but uses X-ray/UV discharge for erase; later EEPROM/Flash electrical-erase mechanisms must be separately sourced rather than projected backward;
133. **historical `read-only` ≠ physical unalterability** — the period phrase `electrically programmable read only memory` names a normal read-service role that coexists with separate programming and erase interventions; ROM vocabulary must be interpreted through the sourced operating regime;
134. **invention priority must be mechanism-specific** — Kahng's 1967-filed Bell Labs patent already describes trapped-charge floating-gate memory, and Frohman's own 1970-filed patent cites the Kahng/Sze work. The Intel sources ground a practical avalanche-injection/radiation-discharge design and decoded programmable array, not a universal `first floating-gate memory` claim;
135. **electrical erasure ≠ ordinary read-service equivalence** — Intel's 2816 brings erase into the electrical/in-system control path, yet READ, BYTE ERASE, BYTE WRITE, CHIP ERASE, and inhibit remain separate modes; erase/write uses an elevated 21 V VPP and millisecond-scale timing rather than the ordinary lower-voltage read path;
136. **electrical erasability ≠ one erase geometry** — the same 2816 supports selected byte erase without affecting adjacent bytes and a separate whole-chip erase mode; `electrical erase` therefore does not determine a unique granularity of forgetting;
137. **electrical erasability ≠ erase-free overwrite** — Intel's 2816 write procedure requires the selected byte to be erased before the intended data is written, so deliberate forgetting can become a constitutive stage of update even when both stages are electrically controlled;
138. **nonvolatility ≠ unlimited mutability** — the 2816 retains state without periodic refresh but is specified for applications requiring up to `1 × 10^4` erase/write cycles per byte; quiescent durability and repeated rewrite endurance are different properties;
139. **value-history forgetting ≠ physical stress-history irrelevance** — the 2816 does not retain an application-visible log of prior byte values, yet repeated erase/write events consume a finite material cycling budget and therefore continue to matter to future state-change reliability;
140. **product operating semantics ≠ exact patent embodiment or invention priority** — the 2816 manufacturer document grounds the named product's FLOTOX/byte/chip-erase behavior while US4203158A grounds a contemporary Intel tunneling mechanism class; neither source justifies silently declaring the patent drawing the exact 2816 production cell, and the patent's own prior-art discussion blocks a universal `Intel invented EEPROM` claim;
141. **program addressability ≠ erase addressability** — Intel's 1988-filed Flash design uses X/Y address and data paths for programming while its erase generator applies the erase condition to the entire array; the unit that can be selected for creation/change need not be the unit that must be forgotten together;
142. **electrical erasability ≠ fine-grained erasability** — Case 12 grounds byte-level electrical erase in the Intel 2816, while Case 13 grounds a whole-array electrical-erase Flash regime; moving erase into electrical control does not determine the scope of erase authority;
143. **fast bulk erase ≠ fast arbitrary rewrite** — Kynett et al.'s 1988 indexed abstract reports whole-array erase on the order of 200 ms and byte programming on the order of 100 µs typical; changing one logical value can still require preservation/reconstruction of other current values sharing the erase domain;
144. **coarse physical state change ≠ coarse verification granularity** — Intel's whole-array erase algorithm walks addresses afterward to verify erased state, repeats erase pulses when necessary, and can report bounded failure; collective state change and local proof of success are separate relations;
145. **one-transistor density objective can be coupled to shared erase authority without becoming a universal law** — Toshiba's 1980-priority patent explicitly contrasts a two-transistor E²PROM density penalty with a one-transistor electrically erasable cell and shared erase/control infrastructure, while its own alternative groupings and cited prior art prevent universalizing that arrangement;
146. **erase asymmetry ≠ FTL** — the early Flash sources establish a physical/control mismatch between fine read/program selection and coarse erase, creating the retention problem to which later copy/reclaim/remap systems respond; they do not establish a Flash Translation Layer, which remains historically separate in Case 04;
147. **logical-block identity ≠ physical-sector identity** — Chan's 1990-filed account explicitly distinguishes the host LBA from the physical target and shows grown-defect replacement redirecting the same logical block to a spare sector; a stable designation need not imply one permanent disk-surface location;
148. **reassignment continuity ≠ payload continuity** — Seagate's `REASSIGN BLOCKS` changes the physical medium serving an LBA but explicitly does not preserve the affected block's data; address continuity and value continuity must therefore be audited separately;
149. **logical-address preservation can constrain repair strategy** — Chan's account rejects renumbering already-used subsequent LBAs because it would cause lost data or misreads, motivating spare substitution while preserving the host-visible address sequence;
150. **defect metadata can be constitutive retention state** — PDL/SDL or equivalent replacement state participates in resolving an LBA to the current physical target and is itself loaded from disk at startup in the bounded Chan account;
151. **repair capacity can be finite hidden infrastructure** — Seagate permits repeated reassignment of one LBA across multiple physical addresses only until spare locations are exhausted; `NO DEFECT SPARE LOCATION AVAILABLE` turns reserved spare space into an explicit continuation limit;
152. **LBA abstraction ≠ disappearance of physical geometry or equivalence to FTL** — period disk sources retain physical target / cylinder-head-sector defect descriptions below a logical-block interface, and the defect-triggered spare substitution in this case remains historically and operationally distinct from Case 04's erase-driven Flash relocation/reclamation;
153. **nonvolatile medium ≠ every necessary current device state is already nonvolatile** — Intel's SSD 320 design explicitly places user/system data in temporary buffers as well as NAND, so the nonvolatility of the final medium does not erase a volatile durability window inside the device;
154. **explicit flush can define a volatile → nonvolatile completion boundary without defining every write semantic** — the 2007 ATA8-ACS working draft requires successful `FLUSH CACHE` completion only after volatile-cache data reach nonvolatile media or an error occurs, while the case deliberately avoids inventing a stronger ordinary-write acknowledgement rule;
155. **explicit flush ≠ orderly-shutdown handoff ≠ unexpected-power-loss emergency handoff** — ATA `FLUSH CACHE`, Intel's `STANDBY IMMEDIATE` shutdown path, and the SSD 320 power-fail interrupt/capacitor path can all move state toward NAND, but their triggers, authority, and failure assumptions differ;
156. **stored energy ≠ stored payload, while stored energy can still be retention infrastructure** — the SSD 320's power-loss-protection capacitance retains energy, not user data, yet that finite energy reserve is constitutive of finishing a transfer into NAND after input power begins to collapse;
157. **payload programming ≠ mapping/currentness durability** — Case 04 establishes mapping as retained state and FAST '13 supplies an independent controller-level counterexample in which programmed payload and validity/mapping consistency can diverge under power fault; this is a cross-case durability distinction, not an Intel-320-specific failure claim;
158. **interface contract or manufacturer feature claim ≠ empirical implementation compliance** — ATA text and Intel's product disclosure establish specified semantics/design intent, while independent fault injection remains a separate evidence class; anonymized FAST '13 results cannot be reassigned to the SSD 320;
159. **application-visible current state ≠ crash-admissible stable state** — 4.4BSD soft updates lets applications see the newest in-memory metadata while dependency enforcement can write an older or temporarily rolled-back version that remains consistent with the current on-disk image; `current` must be qualified by observer and crash contract;
160. **crash-safe mountability ≠ permanence of the latest operation** — the bounded soft-updates filesystem can be safely mounted immediately after a crash while recent delayed updates disappear; structural integrity and recency durability are different retention targets;
161. **consistency-control metadata ≠ necessarily persistent recovery metadata** — soft-updates dependency structures are primarily in-memory machinery used before a crash to constrain what is allowed onto disk; their effect can be persistent even though the control structures themselves are not a separate durable recovery log;
162. **write ordering can preserve crash invariants without a persistent undo/redo journal** — the bounded soft-updates mechanism uses dependency tracking plus temporary rollback/roll-forward of disk-bound metadata versions; this is one historical regime, not a claim that ordering replaces logging universally;
163. **`fsync` durability ≠ payload-block completion** — in the bounded production FFS implementation, committing a file can require allocation bitmaps, data blocks, indirect blocks, the inode, and unwritten directory/name state; the durable object is relational rather than reducible to its payload sectors;
164. **crash admissibility ≠ leak-free resource accounting** — unclaimed blocks/inodes and conservatively high link counts can remain while the on-disk filesystem is safe for immediate service; later/background reclamation is a separate convergence task;
165. **filesystem durability closure ≠ device flush boundary** — Case 15 grounds a lower ATA volatile-cache → nonvolatile-media relation, while Case 16 grounds higher-level dependency/order closure; a device can durably store individually issued writes without thereby deciding which filesystem combinations are crash-admissible, and filesystem semantics cannot substitute for the device’s own durability contract;
166. **parity redundancy ≠ replica multiplicity** — Ouchi's 1977-filed arrangement reconstructs one missing record segment from surviving segments plus an XOR check sum; no surviving storage unit needs to hold a complete duplicate of the missing contribution;
167. **single-failure service continuity ≠ restored redundancy margin** — Chen et al.'s demand reconstruction can satisfy accesses while a member remains failed, while a separate background process reconstructs the full failed-disk contents; current service and repaired fault tolerance are distinct retention targets;
168. **redundant bytes physically present ≠ usable current redundancy** — Chen et al. show parity becoming inconsistent after interrupted writes and require consistency state/regeneration; a physically readable parity sector can fail the currentness condition required for safe reconstruction;
169. **reconstruction progress can be constitutive retention meta state** — Chen et al. explicitly include how much of a failed disk has been reconstructed among state that must be accurately maintained across crashes; the repair frontier is part of the current array condition rather than merely an operator narrative;
170. **spare capacity ≠ stored payload, while spare capacity can be retention infrastructure** — stand-by spare disks enable demand reconstruction and background restoration even though unused spare space does not yet embody current user data;
171. **RAID reconstruction ≠ RADOS replica repair or HDD sector reassignment** — parity reconstruction can synthesize a missing contribution from a coded group; RADOS repairs from version/currentness-qualified replicas; Case 14's `REASSIGN BLOCKS` changes physical serving location but explicitly does not itself preserve damaged payload. Their similarity is functional at the repair/relocation layer, not historical or mechanistic identity;
172. **RAID nomenclature ≠ invention priority for parity-based disk recovery** — Patterson, Gibson, and Katz ground the 1987–1988 `RAID` name/taxonomy, while Ouchi’s IBM filing from 1977 directly grounds an earlier XOR/check-sum multi-device recovery mechanism; terminology history and mechanism priority must remain separate;
173. **physical presence/readability ≠ verified current integrity** — ZFS's checksum relation can reject a returned block even when underlying I/O yields bytes; material survival and filesystem-level admissibility are separate retention relations;
174. **redundancy availability ≠ defect discovery** — a trustworthy alternate copy can coexist with an unknown damaged copy; until demand access or scrubbing exposes the defect, the system may not know that repair is required;
175. **detection work ≠ repair work** — a scrub performs proactive traversal/verification, while repair is conditional on discovering a problem and having a trustworthy source; a scrub can finish with no repairs, expose an unrepairable fault, or trigger self-healing;
176. **scrub ≠ rebuild/resilver** — scrubbing searches current data for integrity/readability defects not necessarily known in advance, while resilvering reconstructs/copies state already known to need restoration because a device is replaced or out of date;
177. **scheduled maintenance ≠ periodic restoration of every retained state** — unlike DRAM refresh, ZFS scrubbing spends scheduled/proactive work primarily on verification; rewriting is conditional on a detected integrity failure and available redundancy;
178. **nominal redundancy ≠ verified repair margin** — a latent defect can silently consume one path needed for later recovery before the system discovers it; earlier verification can expose and repair the defect while another good embodiment still exists. This is a failure-model-relative engineering conclusion, not a ZFS historical variable;
